## intake-1993#record — Fork-and-Flush: Escaping Idea Basins in Autoresearch Agents

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07447
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance high; categories: autonomous_research, agent_architecture, context_management, swarm_techniques)
- **Disposition**: integrated

### Claims (as verified)

0. Independent runs of the same Copilot-CLI agent plateau at very different scores (p171: 8 runs end 68.3-100.0) even with considerable extra compute; an LLM-judged functional-similarity map (t-STE, 0.85 held-out triplet agreement, 5 tasks) shows runs staying in localized "idea basins", and in-run chat compaction did not prevent it. Observational, not causal.
   - *unaffected*: Holds; add that basin evidence is a judged-similarity map on 5 tasks and job-shop is explicitly excluded from basin claims.
   - anchor `§3 Persistent Variance` @ arXiv:2610.07447v1 (html): "Their final best scores range from 68.3 to 100.0, and the gaps remain wide even after considerable additional compute."
1. Fork-and-flush (fixed schedule): opening round of K=4 branches from empty workspaces; leader continues alone; at 33% and 66% of budget B, K=4 branches (~0.06B each) are forked from the leader workspace with EMPTY chat context (W,C)->(W,empty), best-scoring branch becomes the new leader, others are discarded. Only workspace files carry over. Selection uses the task grader score.
   - *narrowed*: Stage-1 omitted: initial K=4 round from empty workspaces, serial leader phases, 33/66% forced cadence, the 2-of-K "different approach" directive (no measurable effect), the protected-incumbent notice, and that selection is by the task grader.
   - anchor `§5.1 Fork-and-Flush Configuration` @ arXiv:2610.07447v1 (html): "The run opens with an initial round of K{=}4 agents launched from empty workspaces, selecting the highest-scoring candidate as the leader. Two subsequent fork-and-flush rounds are triggered strictly by budget consumption—at 33\% and 66\% of B ."
2. Under matched credit budgets (6k-100k Copilot credits, ~100 credits = US$1) on 13 tasks, normalized mean is 0.78 (FF) vs 0.47 (single-run, 8 runs) vs 0.54 (best-of-4); FF has the best mean on 12/13 tasks and lower normalized SD (0.16 vs 0.26). The ablation covers 11 tasks (not 13): single 0.45, flush-only (K=1) 0.55, fork-only (trunk context kept) 0.57, FF 0.71. The "kernel" task is Anthropic VLIW-simulator take-home, NOT a GPU/CPU inference kernel. Models: GPT-5.6-terra (high) on 11 tasks, GPT-5.5 (xhigh) on kernel and tinyCIFAR; closed models, no code release.
   - *narrowed*: Main table is 13 tasks, ablation is 11 (explains the press disagreement; both numbers are right for their table). FF lost on mean to single-run on LWE-rec. Baseline has 8 single runs vs 5 FF seeds.
   - anchor `§5.3 / Table 2 + §5.2` @ arXiv:2610.07447v1 (html): "The flush-only and fork-only ablations achieve scores of 0.55 and 0.57 respectively, compared to 0.45 for the standard single-run baseline, and 0.71 for the full fork-and-flush method."
3. Plateau-triggered forking was abandoned for a fixed schedule because it rarely fired (15/25 dev runs never triggered); cadence/K sensitivity was not swept systematically (Table 5, dev runs n=1-11).
   - *unaffected*: Confirmed; add that the sensitivity evidence is thin.
   - anchor `App.D Fixed Schedule vs Plateau Detection` @ arXiv:2610.07447v1 (html): "across five tasks, 15 of 25 plateau-triggered runs exhausted their budget before the stall criteria were met."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 Stage-2: read FULL v1 html from disk (single version). Confirmed headline numbers (0.78/0.47/0.54; ablation 0.71/0.57/0.55/0.45). Corrected: 11 vs 13 = ablation vs main table; "kernel" = Anthropic VLIW take-home, not hardware kernel work; added exact schedule (K=4, 33%/66%, ~0.06B per branch, opening round from empty workspaces) and the 2-of-K "different approach" directive; baseline n=8 vs FF n=5; no code release; Table 3 flush-only p150 anomaly (160.80 vs max 135.37 elsewhere). 2026-10-20 Stage-2 close-out, dive-surfaced sources declined (named so they are not lost or re-derived): arXiv:2507.14417 - Cited only in Fork-and-Flush related work (long-context degradation); does not change a conclusion. [wave2 declined].

### Verdict

Fork-and-flush = periodic K=4 fan-out from the best workspace with chat context wiped, best branch wins; headline +66%/+44% is normalized mean over 13 tasks (ablation: 11) on closed GPT-5.x agents under one harness (Copilot CLI), 5 seeds/task. Treat as a design pattern for AK/autopilot restart policy, NOT as evidence it helps kernel search on CPU/GPU: the paper "kernel" task is a VLIW-simulator puzzle with a deterministic cycle counter. Do NOT carry the 11-vs-13 confusion (both correct, different tables), do NOT carry "plateau-triggered works" (it was abandoned), and do not quote task-level gains without the n (5 seeds, SDs up to 29 on AHC014).

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `handoffs/active/autokernel-research-loop.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): [unverified] Add a fork-and-flush restart policy (fixed 33%/66% schedule, K branches scored on correctness-only gates) as an experiment in handoffs/active/autokernel-research-loop.md.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): [unverified] Analyze existing AK/autopilot run journals for between-run score spread to test the basin hypothesis (autopilot-continuous-optimization.md).

## intake-1995#record — Harness^2: Recursive Agent Harnessing for an Open World

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14224
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: agent_architecture, autonomous_research, benchmark_methodology)
- **Disposition**: integrated

### Claims (as verified)

0. Agent performance depends on the execution harness; harness edits can be made per task without ground-truth verifiers.
   - *narrowed*: True of the improvement loop (a guard withholds rubrics/scores from improver sessions) but not 'unverified': selector, editor and reflector are LLM judgments (Claude Opus 4.8) and the paper says contrasts 'do not guarantee correctness'.
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2610.14224v1: "Task-level recursion refines the current task’s harness using a contrastive Propose–Probe–Compose step that reliably guides edits without ground-truth verifiers"
   - anchor `App. A.1 Label` @ arXiv:2610.14224v1: "Harness2 uses behavioral contrasts without immediate or delayed correctness labels. Benchmark scores are reserved for evaluation."
1. Task-level Propose-Probe-Compose runs a reference execution, proposes an invariant edit and a domain-specific edit, probes each under matched conditions, and a composer merges/adopts/keeps; contrasts are treated as evidence, not verified correctness.
   - *unaffected*: Propose-Probe-Compose mechanics re-read in Sec 2.2.1 and App. A.1; contrasts are evidence, not verification (paper's own wording).
   - anchor `§2.2.1 Probe` @ arXiv:2610.14224v1: "as execution stochasticity and shared model biases limit the reliability of the evidence."
   - anchor `§2.2.1 Compose` @ arXiv:2610.14224v1: "Composition is selective: it may merge supported changes, revise conflicting edits, adopt one candidate, or preserve the current harness."
2. Harnessing-level recursion keeps a library of adopted harnesses plus EXPERIENCE.md lessons that later tasks reuse; harness has eight editable components.
   - *unaffected*: Library of adopted harnesses plus EXPERIENCE.md and the eight editable components confirmed in Sec 2.1 and 2.3.
   - anchor `§2.3 Update the Improvement Harness` @ arXiv:2610.14224v1: "Each library entry contains an executable harness and an EXPERIENCE.md recording the applicable situation, lessons, and supporting evidence."
   - anchor `§2.1 Harness Configuration` @ arXiv:2610.14224v1: "a system prompt, guardrails, memory, skills, tool guidance, scripts, subagents, and a middleware plugin."
3. One step lifts GLM-5 and Gemini 3.5 Flash over base harnesses; k=5 recursion adds more (parallel pool or sequential chain, agentic selector).
   - *narrowed*: k=5 gains are real (+2.9..+3.9 WorkBuddy, +3.0..+4.2 JobBench, +2.6..+6.7 LAB over k=1) but the table compares against single-rollout baselines; the App. C best-of-k baseline result is not in the readable text, so matched-budget superiority is not shown. Meta-Harness leads at LAB/Codex/Gemini k=1 (76.5 vs 75.6). One Stage-1 number overturned: reported_results[2] LAB GLM-5 k=5 is 78.1, not 74.4.
   - anchor `§3.2 Main Results` @ arXiv:2610.14224v1: "On WorkBuddy-Bench, Harness2 raises GLM-5’s average score by 11.5 points under OpenCode, the largest one-step improvement over the base harness on this benchmark."
   - anchor `§3.2 Main Results (k=5 deltas)` @ arXiv:2610.14224v1: "adds a further 2.9 to 3.9 points on WorkBuddy-Bench, 3.0 to 4.2 points on JobBench, and 2.6 to 6.7 percentage points in LAB’s Partial pass rate over a single step."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 Stage-2 dive (FULL read of arXiv:2610.14224v1 HTML, sha256 e0c817d9...; repo google-research/harness2 @ 1cfe254e8d25 read PARTIAL: README, docs/EXPERIMENTS.md, docs/SETUP.md, core/library.py, core/proposer.py, substrate/codex.py; core/recursion.py, schedule.py, selection.py, prompts.py bodies and benchmarks/* NOT read; figure pixels NOT read). Changes: (a) reported_results[2] LAB GLM-5 k=5 corrected 74.4 -> 78.1 (Table 2 '+ Recursion' deltas +4.2/-0.5/+6.7/+3.0 = JobBench score/steps, LAB Partial/steps; 74.4 added the #Steps delta); Gemini LAB 79.9 -> 81.9 -> 84.5 and the other reported_results re-derived and unaffected. (b) key_claim 0 narrowed: no ground-truth verifier in the loop, but selector/editor/reflector are LLM judgments. (c) key_claim 3 narrowed: k=5 vs single-rollout baselines, best-of-k result not in readable text. (d) Stage-1 note 'limitations (noisy probes, 3 benchmarks)' narrowed: App. F lists two (cost of recursion; execution feedback sensitive to variability and shared biases). (e) Stage-1 note 'editor/judge is Claude, not self-hostable' narrowed: Sec 4 same-model executor/editor (k=2) reports gains for all five models tested; repo proposer/selector channels are only claude_cli and opencode; a local llama.cpp endpoint via OpenCode was NOT tested; no sub-30B editor evidence. (f) The wavect warning (unrestricted executor network, Codex sandbox disabled) is real but originates in the repo docs (README; docs/EXPERIMENTS.md rubric-download incident; docs/SETUP.md; substrate/codex.py:166-167; core/proposer.py:216); wavect restates them (not independent), and the paper reports no network restriction used, so rubric-retrieval contamination of the Codex-executor rows cannot be ruled out. (g) ID resolved: arxiv.org/abs/2610.14224 is v1 only and all six referenced_arxiv_ids resolve with matching titles. (h) NOVELTY NARROWED (Stage-2b waves 1-2): label-free harness refinement predates Harness^2. TTHE (arXiv 2607.08124, intake-2145#record; label-free execution-derived proxies, batch protocol), RHI (arXiv 2607.15524, intake-2146#record; pairwise output judgments) and Adaptive Auto-Harness (arXiv 2606.01770, intake-2147#record; delayed labels) were read in full, and Harness^2 itself cites them as the proxy, pairwise and delayed-label variants. What remains new: per-TASK recursion with matched contrastive probes of a single edit, a composer that can return KEEP_BASE, and the executable-plus-EXPERIENCE.md library. (i) Stage-1 contradiction note (c) on arXiv 2607.14004 ('gains fail to compound' for both baselines) narrowed: that paper (intake-2143#record; vendor-authored, own method wins, so not independent corroboration) shows GEPA negative transfer after the FIRST optimization pass and GEPA recovering after the second; cite intake-2143#03 and intake-2143#05. (j) Surfaced sources ingested-and-dived as Stage-2b entries: harness2 repo = intake-2148#record, arXiv 2605.30621 = intake-2144#record. Declined for now: arXiv 2608.19013 HCL (concurrent retention-aware variant; no EPYC consumer yet; revisit if HIL needs retention/forgetting handling). credibility_score 3 unchanged; read_depth FULL for the paper, PARTIAL for the repo.

### Verdict

Cite Harness^2 only as a PATTERN source (step record, EXPERIENCE-entry template, scoped proposers with a KEEP_BASE composer, an egress/answer-key isolation item for the HIL-2 checklist). Do not cite its contrast as verification or as a keep/revert/promote gate (an observation under MEASUREMENT_POLICY, usable only to generate, rank or discard candidates). Do not cite it as the origin of label-free harness refinement (TTHE, RHI and AAH predate it). Use LAB GLM-5 k=5 = 78.1, not 74.4. No sub-30B local editor evidence; executor network was unrestricted in the release.

## intake-1998#record — Scaling Discovery Requires Forgetting

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20563
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: autonomous_research, agent_architecture, swarm_techniques)
- **Disposition**: monitor

### Claims (as verified)

0. Single agent, 4 parallel agents and 4 communicating agents all narrow as sessions grow (smaller edits, less reasoning; on the VLIW task 66.2% of single-agent spend went to empty turns declaring a plateau), but the plateau is uneven: on Frontier-CS Q171 parallel agents keep improving and on nanochat parallel gives no gain over a single agent.
   - *narrowed*: Mechanism (anchoring on history, smaller edits) is supported by pooled AHC014 traces and VLIW session logs; "all three plateau" is not uniform (Q171 parallel keeps improving; nanochat parallel = single).
   - anchor `p.7 §5` @ arXiv:2610.20563v1 (PDF, sha256 9c14df83...85edc; text extracted with a local zlib script because pypdf is not installed): "As sessions lengthen, agents make smaller edits and use less reasoning."
1. Forgetting = at fixed round boundaries (three forgets, four rounds, no adaptive trigger) discard every agent session and the whole search state (all candidates, scores, feedback in the workspace) and restart fresh empty-context agents from the single best candidate; no notes, lessons or do-not-repeat records are carried. A 4-agent team also shares candidates as commits (token-free) within rounds.
   - *narrowed*: Discards more than "sessions and candidate history": also the workspace/lessons; fixed cadence; keeps best candidate ONLY. Distinct from Fork-and-Flush (keeps workspace) per the paper itself, p.3.
   - anchor `p.3 §1` @ arXiv:2610.20563v1 (PDF, sha256 9c14df83...85edc; text extracted with a local zlib script because pypdf is not installed): "When an agent forgets, it discards its session, along with all candidate solutions and records in the filesystem state, and keeps only the best candidate."
   - anchor `p.9 §6` @ arXiv:2610.20563v1 (PDF, sha256 9c14df83...85edc; text extracted with a local zlib script because pypdf is not installed): "A natural next step is to decide when to forget and what to remember."
2. Four tasks: AtCoder AHC014 heuristic (C++), Frontier-CS Q171 (C++), Anthropic VLIW take-home (Python kernel for a simulated VLIW SIMD machine, minimize cycles, reference 147,734), nanochat-autoresearch (5-minute PyTorch training on one H100). All with Pi agent + DeepSeek V4.1 Flash, $20-$40 per replicate, identical money across arms, 3 runs per configuration.
   - *narrowed*: "Kernel optimization" is a simulated-machine cycle-count task (deterministic judge), not hardware kernel work; model is DeepSeek V4.1 Flash via Pi agent.
   - anchor `p.13 Table 4` @ arXiv:2610.20563v1 (PDF, sha256 9c14df83...85edc; text extracted with a local zlib script because pypdf is not installed): "Write a kernel for a simulated VLIW SIMD machine that traverses a tree and hashes a batch of values"
3. Table 1 means (3 runs): team-with-forgetting beats 4 parallel agents by +14% (AHC014 score), +15% (Q171), ~10% fewer cycles (VLIW), ~2% lower BPB (nanochat 0.961 vs 0.980); versus a single agent +39% (AHC014), +26% (Q171), -23% cycles (VLIW). The headline +15%/+39% are the Q171 and AHC014 maxima, not the kernel task. The no-forgetting 4-agent TEAM is not a full-budget arm in Table 1; the controlled forget-vs-continue comparison (Table 3) exists only for AHC014 (30 proposals, 3 replicates, team gain 9.2+-6.9% vs 1.4+-1.3%, at 15.32 vs 24.67 USD).
   - *reattributed*: +15% is Q171 (vs parallel), +39% is AHC014 (vs single); kernel-task gains are ~10% vs parallel and 23% vs single on means. n=3 per arm; maxima of different tasks.
   - anchor `p.6 Table 1 (team row)` @ arXiv:2610.20563v1 (PDF, sha256 9c14df83...85edc; text extracted with a local zlib script because pypdf is not installed): "Team with forgetting 1,145 (+19%) 1,103±50 (+39%) 1,077 (-15%) 1,097±18 (-23%)"
   - anchor `p.6 §4.1` @ arXiv:2610.20563v1 (PDF, sha256 9c14df83...85edc; text extracted with a local zlib script because pypdf is not installed): "Frontier-CS Q171 is the exception, where parallel agents keep improving, but the team with forgetting still ends 15% above them."
   - anchor `p.5 §4` @ arXiv:2610.20563v1 (PDF, sha256 9c14df83...85edc; text extracted with a local zlib script because pypdf is not installed): "We ran each configuration three times per task."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 Stage-2: PDF read in full (text extracted locally; HTML 404). Overturned the reading of the headline: +15%/+39% are Q171 and AHC014 means, not the kernel task; kernel task is a simulated VLIW machine (kernel gains ~10% vs parallel, 23% vs single, means over 3 runs). Forgetting also discards the workspace and keeps no notes; cadence is fixed (3 forgets / 4 rounds); n=3 per arm; controlled forget-vs-continue only on AHC014; relevance lowered high -> medium.

### Verdict

Take away: one lab (UC Berkeley, 3 runs/arm, one cheap open model, deterministic cheap judges) shows that discarding ALL accumulated agent state except the best candidate, at fixed round boundaries, beat continuing sessions at lower cost on AHC014 and gave the best mean on all four tasks. Do NOT carry: "+15% / +39% on kernel optimization" (those are Q171 and AHC014); any claim about GPU/CPU kernels (the kernel task is a simulated VLIW machine); any claim that the effect transfers to noisy, expensive real-hardware judges like AK or to a frontier planner model; any adaptive trigger (none exists). No independent replication found; hardware-free so it is cheap to test only as a process change, not a benchmark.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `handoffs/active/autokernel-research-loop.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): [unverified] Read full body, then evaluate periodic restart-from-best in AutoKernel supervisor; owner handoffs/active/autokernel-research-loop.md

## intake-1999#record — Recurrent Harness for Agentic Continual Learning

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12668
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: memory_augmented, agent_architecture, context_management)
- **Disposition**: integrated

### Claims (as verified)

0. ReHarness adds a backward revision step to reflection-memory harnesses: after each instance, the retrieved reflections are rewritten using the new trajectory as feedback, grounded in their source trajectories.
   - *unaffected*: Grounded rate >95% (leakage 0.8-2.1%, unsupported 0.5-2.6%) per Sec 5.1, measured with a GPT-5.6-Sol claim decomposition on CL-Bench only (LLM auditing LLM revisions). Revised R'_i REPLACES R_i (overwrite semantics).
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2610.12668v1: "after each instance, we leverage the resulting trajectory as feedback to revise the reflections retrieved for that instance, thereby propagating feedback backward through the textual harness state."
   - anchor `§3.3 Step 2` @ arXiv:2610.12668v1: "Any new content in Ri′R^{\prime}_{i} must remain grounded in its source trajectory τi\tau_{i} to prevent information leakage."
1. Strictly online: no replay, validation set or gradient updates; top-5 retrieval via Qwen3-Embedding-8B.
   - *narrowed*: 'Strictly online' confirmed, but every instance returns an environment reward r_t (Sec 3.1) and the revision agent uses it: ReHarness is validation-set-free, NOT verifier-free. Poker ('noisy signal for backward revision') is the paper's own failure case.
   - anchor `§1` @ arXiv:2610.12668v1: "This enables online harness adaptation under a strictly streaming protocol without instance replay or gradient-based parameter updates."
   - anchor `§4.1 Baselines` @ arXiv:2610.12668v1: "Retrieval uses the top five entries ranked by Qwen3-Embedding-8B (Zhang et al., 2025a) and applies the same query-to-query comparison across all methods."
2. Beats raw-context, memory/reflection and prompt-optimisation baselines (Mem0, RBank, DoT, ACE, GEPA-Online) across three benchmarks and three proprietary backbones.
   - *unaffected*: 0.240 vs 0.168/0.164 re-derived from Table 1; first-or-tied in 8/9 (CL-Bench/Gemini 0.233 vs 0.234 is the one miss) but statistically marginal: padj 0.039 and 0.043, Wilcoxon over three seeded rollouts.
   - anchor `§4.2 Overall Performance` @ arXiv:2610.12668v1: "ReHarness achieves the highest overall normalized gain of 0.2400.240 and ranks first or tied in eight of the nine combinations."
   - anchor `§4.2 Overall Performance` @ arXiv:2610.12668v1: "Top-K Raw (padj=0.039p_{\mathrm{adj}}=0.039) and Full Raw (padj=0.043p_{\mathrm{adj}}=0.043)"
   - anchor `§4.1 Baselines` @ arXiv:2610.12668v1: "We evaluate all methods with three frontier LLM backbones: Claude Opus 4.8 (Anthropic, 2026), GPT-5.5 (OpenAI, 2026a), and Gemini 3.7 Flash (Google DeepMind, 2026), using the same backbone for all agents within each method."
3. Token-cheaper than full raw context; weak on noisy-reward tasks (poker).
   - *narrowed*: Tokens ~41.57M vs 52.66M (Top-K Raw) vs 142.12M (Full Raw), one rollout; with caching the dollar order is Top-K Raw $85 < ReHarness $254 < Full Raw $314, so ReHarness is ~3.0x Top-K Raw in dollars (4.38M vs 0.46M output tokens) and needs raw-trajectory storage.
   - anchor `§4.2 Token Efficiency` @ arXiv:2610.12668v1: "With prompt caching and Claude API pricing, the corresponding costs are approximately $85\$85 for Top-K Raw, $254\$254 for ReHarness, and $314\$314 for Full Raw."
   - anchor `§4.2 Token Efficiency` @ arXiv:2610.12668v1: "ReHarness therefore remains substantially cheaper than Full Raw while achieving a higher normalized gain"
   - anchor `§4.2 Task-Level Results` @ arXiv:2610.12668v1: "By contrast, ReHarness is less effective when downstream rewards provide a noisy signal for backward revision, and benefits most when feedback is diagnostic for revising reusable reflections."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 Stage-2 dive (FULL read of arXiv:2610.12668v1 HTML sha256 777a79b0...; no code release found; Figure 5 prompts and Figs 6-7 examples are images, so the revision-agent prompt text was NOT read). Changes: (a) key_claim 1 narrowed: not verifier-free, it consumes a per-instance reward (validation-set-free only). (b) key_claim 3 narrowed: cheaper than Full Raw but ~3x the dollar cost of Top-K Raw. (c) key_claim 2 unaffected but statistically marginal (padj 0.039/0.043, three rollouts, strongest baselines close). (d) reported_results[2] ablation confirmed with scope added: CL-Bench, Claude only; 'No Revision' is 0.144 vs 0.217, the 0.191 figure is 'without revised demonstrations'; the counterfactual replay 0.335 'violates the online streaming protocol' (upper-bound diagnostic, not an online method). (e) Backbones confirmed: Claude Opus 4.8, GPT-5.5, Gemini 3.7 Flash; embeddings Qwen3-Embedding-8B; MemoryBench judge GPT-5.6-Sol. (f) Stage-1 corroboration note on arXiv 2607.14004 ('methods assume repeatable tasks and reliable verifiers') was NOT verified against the paper text by this dive; that paper (intake-2143#record) was dived in Stage-2b wave 1 and is related evidence (GEPA, Meta-Harness, its own RELAI-VCL), not a replication of ReHarness. Surfaced sources ingested-and-dived as Stage-2b entries: Continual Learning Bench = arXiv 2606.05661 = intake-2149#record; ACE = existing intake-1071#record (re-encounter note added there). Declined for now: MemoryArena / MemoryBench benchmarks (our instruments are M-12/BEAM/UTM; revisit only if the R1 spec needs an external stream). credibility_score 3 unchanged (lower end).

### Verdict

Adopt the pattern ONLY where a measured per-instance reward exists: ReHarness is validation-set-free, not verifier-free, and fails on noisy-reward tasks (poker ~0.009). Its headline is statistically marginal (padj 0.039/0.043, three rollouts). Do not claim it is cheaper than Top-K Raw in dollars ($254 vs $85). No code released and the revision prompts were not readable; all backbones are proprietary, so open-model behaviour is unknown.

## intake-2000#record — RSI-Anything: Unlocking Recursive Self-Improvement with World Models

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13912
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance low; categories: autonomous_research, agent_architecture, training_distillation)
- **Disposition**: monitor
- **Expanded from**: intake-1980#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. The pre-screening predictor in the controlled code-repair experiment is a trained 3B model (W1, LoRA on Qwen2.5-Coder-3B) giving five-class execution-outcome probabilities per candidate-test pair; the policy proposes 4 revisions and the predictor picks 1 to execute. Feedback labels come from executing the chosen candidate; adaptation is triggered only by a reliability check.
   - *narrowed*: Selection quality at constant evaluation count, not fewer evaluations.
   - anchor `§6.1 Code repair (W1 predictor)` @ arXiv:2610.13912v1 (html): "W1 is our 3B execution-outcome world model, built using the predictive design of JEPA-Anything Cui et al. (2026b) and adapted from Qwen2.5-Coder-3B-Instruct Hui et al. (2024) with LoRA Hu et al. (2022)."
   - anchor `§3.1 Table 2 paragraph` @ arXiv:2610.13912v1 (html): "Online test-suite usage was similar: 3.71 calls per task for the fixed-world-model configuration and 3.72 for the static verifier."
   - anchor `§3.1` @ arXiv:2610.13912v1 (html): "The paired gain over the static verifier was +2.83 percentage points (95% interval [+0.17,+5.50])."
1. Evaluation-count savings are not measured: online test calls are ~3.7 per task for every arm (random 3.72, static verifier 3.72, fixed WM 3.71, adapted 3.72). Hidden all-pass is 26.0 / 26.3 / 29.2 / 31.2 percent; the fixed-WM gain over the static verifier is +2.83 pp with 95% interval [+0.17,+5.50] over 600 task-seed runs.
   - *unaffected*: Applies to imagined-trajectory training admission.
   - anchor `§2.3 Evidence and imagined training data` @ arXiv:2610.13912v1 (html): "RSI-Anything distinguishes deterministic rule checks (E0), learned-model predictions and rollouts (E1), specialized simulators or higher-fidelity calculations (E2), and external measurements or experiments (E3)."
2. Evidence tiers E0-E3 govern admission of imagined trajectories into policy training; policy, world model and the pair are gated separately and retained only on matched-condition checks. No ablation isolates tiers, pair gating, or the JEPA/OPF design.
   - *unaffected*: Confirmed.
   - anchor `§2.4 Conditional world-model adaptation` @ arXiv:2610.13912v1 (html): "Insufficient coverage yields an insufficient assessment rather than evidence of reliability and does not activate the adaptation trigger."
3. Reaction-yield and lab-planning gains rest on domain-specific pretrained predictors and, for lab planning, a comparison across successive policy versions rather than predictor on/off; the paper's own discussion says joint multiround evaluation is not yet established.
   - *narrowed*: Add: CI lower bound +0.17; adapted-vs-fixed no CI; no surrogate on/off ablation outside code repair.
   - anchor `§5 Discussion` @ arXiv:2610.13912v1 (html): "Joint multiround evaluation is needed to establish sustained mutual improvement of the policy and world model within a single task."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 Stage-2: predictor is a trained 3B classifier (not a prompted LLM); no evaluation savings are reported (calls/task flat); gain CI is [+0.17,+5.50]; no ablation of tiers/pair gating; figures 3c,d,e,f and appendix not separately read as images (text of captions read). Stage-1 claim 3 'each domain is small-scale' stands.

### Verdict

Do NOT cite this paper as evidence that a learned surrogate cuts the number of expensive trials; it shows a trained task-specific predictor improving which of 4 candidates gets run at equal evaluation cost, with a small effect (+2.83 pp, CI lower bound +0.17) on a single benchmark. For AutoKernel take only the design pattern (predict, then verify with the real measurement; gate predictor updates on a reliability check; keep prediction and observation provenance separate), and treat whether it pays off for kernel trials as an empirical question answerable by an offline replay on AK's own history.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): [unverified] Note evidence-tier + pair-retention gating pattern for handoffs/active/autopilot-continuous-optimization.md; no build work.

## intake-2001#record — PersonaPlex: Voice and Role Control for Full Duplex Conversational Speech Models

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06053
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: multimodal, local_inference, training_distillation)
- **Disposition**: knowledge_only
- **Expanded from**: intake-1982#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. PersonaPlex adds role (text) and voice (audio sample) conditioning to Moshi through a hybrid system prompt placed before the dialogue (user channel replaced with a 440 Hz sine while the prompt plays).
   - *unaffected*: CONFIRMED: s3.1: Hybrid System Prompt = text prompt segment + voice prompt segment; user channel replaced by a 440 Hz sine during the prompt; custom delimiters.
   - anchor `s3.1 Architecture` @ arXiv:2602.06053v1 (html): "For stable conditioning, the user audio channel is replaced with a 440 Hz sine wave, and custom text/audio delimiters mark the boundary between the Hybrid System Prompt and dialogue."
1. It is a Moshi fine-tune (initialised from Moshi weights) trained 24,576 steps at batch 32, 2048-token windows (163.84 s) in 6 h on 8xA100, on 1,840 h / 105,410 customer-service dialogs plus 410 h / 39,322 QA dialogs, all synthetic (text: Qwen-3-32B, GPT-OSS-120B; audio: Dia, Chatterbox).
   - *narrowed*: Add the data generators and the exact training data volumes; 'open-source LLMs and TTS' = Qwen-3-32B, GPT-OSS-120B, Dia, Chatterbox.
   - anchor `s4 Experiments and results` @ arXiv:2602.06053v1 (html): "Training takes 6 hours on 8xA100 GPUs."
2. Service-Duplex-Bench = 50 service roles x 7 single-turn probes (350 questions) added to FDB's 400; scored with a GPT-4o judge. Its public release is only promised (paper: 'We plan to release'); no copy found in the FDB repo.
   - *narrowed*: Benchmark data release is promised, not evidenced; Stage-1 listed it as an extension without caveat.
   - anchor `s3.3 Service-Duplex-Bench` @ arXiv:2602.06053v1 (html): "We plan to release this dataset to provide an evaluation framework for future models."
3. Superiority is partial: best open model on speaker similarity (0.57 vs Moshi 0.10; released checkpoint 0.65; Gemini 0.00 because its voice is fixed) and turn-taking latency, but Gemini Live wins role adherence (4.73 vs 4.48), pause handling and backchannel TOR; Moshi is faster at interruption (0.257 s vs 0.400 s); DMOS differences vs Gemini sit inside overlapping 95% CIs.
   - *narrowed*: Superiority is not uniform across dimensions (see corrected claim 3); Moshi beats it on interruption latency; naturalness gap vs Gemini is within CI.
   - anchor `s4.2` @ arXiv:2602.06053v1 (html): "Furthermore, on the Service-Duplex-Bench benchmark, which focuses on role adherence and instruction following, PersonaPlex outperforms all models except Gemini Live."
   - anchor `Table 2 (Gemini row)` @ arXiv:2602.06053v1 (html): "Gemini | 0.255 | 0.310 | 0.091 | 0.012 | 0.896 | 0.655 | 1.301 | 0.891 | 3.376 | 1.183"
4. Table 2 (experimental model, FDB v1 tasks, latency in seconds per the FDB paper): pause TOR 0.584/0.662 (synthetic/Candor, lower better), backchannel TOR 0.327, Freq 0.025, JSD 0.649, smooth-turn TOR 0.992 / latency 0.070, interruption TOR 1.000 / GPT-4o 4.210 / latency 0.400. Released checkpoint (Table 6) is different: pause TOR 0.358/0.431, backchannel Freq 0.042, JSD 0.662, smooth-turn TOR 0.908 / latency 0.170, interruption TOR 0.950 / GPT-4o 4.290 / latency 0.240. The HF card defines interruption latency as time until the agent STOPS speaking whereas the FDB paper defines it as time to respond after the interruption; the PersonaPlex paper does not state units.
   - anchor `Table 2 (PersonaPlex row)` @ arXiv:2602.06053v1 (html): "PersonaPlex | 0.584 | 0.662 | 0.327 | 0.025 | 0.649 | 0.992 | 0.070 | 1.000 | 4.210 | 0.400"
   - anchor `Table 6 (Released row)` @ arXiv:2602.06053v1 (html): "PersonaPlex (Released) | 0.358 | 0.431 | 0.273 | 0.042 | 0.662 | 0.908 | 0.170 | 0.950 | 4.290 | 0.240"
   - anchor `HF model card, FullDuplexBench Benchmark Scores` @ HF nvidia/personaplex-7b-v1@fdaf4090a61cb315c138a1faee287ffd6c716309 (rendered blob page; raw README is gated): "User Interruption: Latency↓ | 0.240"
5. DMOS (crowd MOS, 95% CI): FDB interruption set PersonaPlex 3.90+-0.15, Gemini 3.72+-0.14, Qwen-2.5-Omni 3.70+-0.13, Freeze-Omni 3.51+-0.18, Moshi 3.11+-0.15; Service-Duplex-Bench 3.59+-0.12 vs Gemini 3.22+-0.14.
   - anchor `Table 1` @ arXiv:2602.06053v1 (html): "PersonaPlex | 3.90±0.15 | 3.59±0.12 | 0.57"
6. Role adherence (GPT-4o mean over Q0-Q6, Table 4): Gemini 4.73, PersonaPlex 4.48, Freeze-Omni 4.02, Qwen-2.5-Omni 2.76, Moshi 1.75. Data scaling (Table 5) is not monotone on FDB: GPT-4o 4.21 at 100% data vs 4.52 at 50% and 4.44 at 25%; SSIM 0.10 -> 0.54 (25%) -> 0.56 (50%) -> 0.57 (100%).
   - anchor `Table 4` @ arXiv:2602.06053v1 (html): "PersonaPlex | 4.6 | 4.6 | 4.4 | 4.5 | 4.5 | 4.3 | 4.5 | 4.48"
   - anchor `Table 5` @ arXiv:2602.06053v1 (html): "50% | 0.56 | 4.52 | 4.24"
7. No model size, VRAM, hardware, or inference-latency-per-frame figure is given in the paper; serving facts come from intake-1982#record.
   - anchor `s4` @ arXiv:2602.06053v1 (html): "The full training dataset has 1840 hours of customer service dialog interactions across 105,410 dialogs, and 410 hours of general Question-Answering dialogs across 39,322 dialogs."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 Stage-2: every number in the Stage-1 reported_results verified against Tables 1,2,4,5,6; units resolved to seconds via the FDB paper; added the experimental-vs-released table split; superiority claim narrowed (Gemini wins role adherence, pause TOR, backchannel TOR; Moshi wins interruption latency; DMOS CIs overlap); Service-Duplex-Bench release not evidenced; data-scaling non-monotonicity on FDB GPT-4o noted; interruption-latency definition conflict between card and FDB paper noted.

### Verdict

PersonaPlex is an NVIDIA-authored Moshi fine-tune whose paper reports FDB numbers for an EXPERIMENTAL model; the released checkpoint behaves differently (Table 6) and the HF card quotes only the released numbers. Do not cite Table 2 as the released model's performance, and do not say it beats Gemini overall (it loses role adherence, pause handling and backchannel TOR; DMOS differences are within CI). Latency units are seconds (from the FDB paper, not stated in this one). The paper offers no hardware/VRAM/model-size data, so any serving claim must come from intake-1982#record.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): [unverified] Reuse Full-Duplex-Bench / Service-Duplex-Bench as the turn-taking gate in the CS-5 reference harness.

## intake-2002#record — Fish Audio Drama 3 Technical Report

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18010
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: multimodal, moe_optimization, ssm_hybrid, inference_serving)
- **Disposition**: monitor
- **Expanded from**: intake-1984#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Drama 3 is a directed-performance TTS (multi-reference cloning, natural-language direction, multi-turn revision, multi-character scenes, infilling, long-form and streaming text input) in one model; the 16-speaker limit appears on the HF card only; the report's 'open-source' label means published open weights (see intake-1984#record).
   - *narrowed*: 16-speaker limit not in report.
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2610.18010v2 (html): "We introduce Fish Audio Drama 3, an open-source speech generation model that turns text into directed performances, built on a 35B-parameter mixture-of-experts backbone that activates 3B parameters per token."
1. Backbone: Qwen3.5-35B-A3B language model (40 layers; each block = 3 Gated DeltaNet + 1 gated full-attention, i.e. 10 full-attention layers; 8 of 256 experts routed + shared expert; ~3B of 35B active). Qwen3.5's own MTP layer is replaced.
   - *narrowed*: Layer pattern is 3 DeltaNet : 1 full attention per 4-layer block (Stage-1 inverted it).
   - anchor `s2.2 Backbone` @ arXiv:2610.18010v2 (html): "a 40-layer hybrid that stacks blocks of three Gated DeltaNet layers (Yang et al., 2025), a gated form of linear attention (Katharopoulos et al., 2020; Yang et al., 2024), and one gated full-attention layer"
2. Audio: S2 tokenizer (DAC-based, 44.1 kHz, 10 codebooks, ~21.5 frames/s; fully causal); only codebooks 0 and 1 are fed back to the backbone; a 13-layer codebook-MTP decoder (4 shared + 9 per-codebook full-width layers attending causally over the whole sequence) predicts the other nine codebooks, running once per frame.
   - *unaffected*: CONFIRMED: s2.4: 13 layers = 4 shared + 9 per-codebook; s2.1: S2 tokenizer on Descript Audio Codec, 44.1 kHz, ten codebooks at ~21.5 frames/s.
   - anchor `s2.4 Codebook-MTP Decoder` @ arXiv:2610.18010v2 (html): "The decoder stacks 13 layers of one design, with four shared layers followed by nine per-codebook layers, one for each acoustic codebook."
3. Training: three pre-training stages (sequences to 8K then 16K tokens, Muon), instruction SFT to 32K tokens, then GRPO (G=8, no KL penalty, 1,536 prompts/step, rollout experts in block-scaled FP8) driven by ONE learned human-preference reward model; the codebook-MTP decoder is frozen during RL.
   - *unaffected*: Add: decoder frozen in RL; GRPO details.
   - anchor `s3.3 Policy optimization` @ arXiv:2610.18010v2 (html): "The rollout engines run the routed experts in block-scaled 8-bit floating point (FP8)"
4. Serving numbers are NVIDIA-only: 271 frames/s single-stream on one H200 vs 177 for Fish Audio S2 measured the same way (codec needs 21.5 frames/s for real time); 295 frames/s on B300 with MXFP8 experts; prefill-decode disaggregation: 24 streams per GPU (averaged over the prefill+decode pair, bar = no stall and >= 35 frames/s at the first percentile) vs ~10 on a single RTX PRO 6000. The report states NO first-audio/TTFA figure (grep: none); TTFA exists only on the HF card (intake-1984#record).
   - anchor `s4.1` @ arXiv:2610.18010v2 (html): "At a concurrency of 1, Drama 3 decodes 271 frames per second on one NVIDIA H200 GPU (NVIDIA Corporation, 2026a), against 177 for S2 measured the same way."
   - anchor `s4.4` @ arXiv:2610.18010v2 (html): "Under this bar, a unit of one prefill and one decode GPU carries 24 streams per GPU, averaged over both, against about 10 for a single GPU."
5. Quality: Seed-TTS-Eval test-zh CER 0.83 and test-en WER 1.54 are AFTER regeneration of takes that fail signal checks (silent/too little speech/runaway); first-take values are 1.00 and 2.20 (Table 4). Streaming WER 1.86 vs 1.83 non-streamed (SIM 0.640 both) was measured on the 1,088 Seed-TTS-Eval test-en sentences, NOT LibriSpeech-PC.
   - anchor `Table 4` @ arXiv:2610.18010v2 (html): "Fish Audio Drama 3 | 1.00 | 0.83 | 2.20 | 1.54 | 1.24 | 1.84"
   - anchor `s5.4 Streaming Synthesis` @ arXiv:2610.18010v2 (html): "We do this for all 1,088 Seed-TTS-Eval test-en sentences. Streaming costs no quality: on the same sentences, streamed takes reach a WER of 1.86 and non-streamed takes 1.83, and both reach a SIM of 0.640."
6. On LibriSpeech-PC test-clean (Table 5, one reference, baselines quoted from OmniVoice): Drama 3 WER 1.85 / SIM 0.605 / UTMOS 4.31 versus Qwen3-TTS (the basis of our production qwentts.cpp) WER 1.60 / SIM 0.704 / UTMOS 4.41 and CosyVoice 3 1.59 / 0.694 / 4.28, so Drama 3 is not better than Qwen3-TTS on plain cloning there; its claimed edge is directability, editing and multilingual error rates (MiniMax testset, Table 6).
   - anchor `Table 5` @ arXiv:2610.18010v2 (html): "Qwen3-TTS (Hu et al., 2026) | 1.60 | 0.704 | 4.41"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 Stage-2: layer pattern corrected (3 DeltaNet + 1 full attention per block); streaming WER dataset corrected (Seed-TTS-Eval test-en, not LibriSpeech-PC); Seed-TTS-Eval figures annotated as post-regeneration with first-take values; 16-speaker and TTFA statements shown to be card-only; new finding that Qwen3-TTS beats Drama 3 on WER/SIM/UTMOS in Table 5; PD '24 streams per GPU' qualified by its stall/frame-rate bar.

### Verdict

The report is vendor-authored, H200/B300-only, and does not publish first-audio latency. Its architecture (hybrid DeltaNet+MoE backbone, codebook-MTP decoder, FP8 experts) is technically interesting for kernel reuse thinking, but it gives no CPU or MI210 number and no llama.cpp path. Do NOT cite 'first audio ~89 ms' as a report result (card only), do NOT cite 1.54/0.83 as raw model error rates (post-regeneration), and do NOT say Drama 3 beats Qwen3-TTS on cloning - Table 5 shows the opposite on LibriSpeech-PC.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `handoffs/active/conversation-stack.md`

## intake-2003#record — Full-Duplex-Bench: A Benchmark to Evaluate Full-duplex Spoken Dialogue Models on Turn-taking Capabilities

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04721
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_component (novelty medium, relevance high; categories: benchmark_methodology, multimodal)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1982#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. FDB v1 tests four behaviours of a model treated as a black box that receives a time-synchronous input.wav stream and emits output.wav, which is ASR-aligned (parakeet-tdt-0.6b-v2) and scored: pause handling (user pauses 0.4-1.0 s mid-turn; model should NOT take over), backchanneling (<1 s and <2 words counts as backchannel), smooth turn-taking (response latency after the user ends, gaps <0.4 s), and user interruption (response to a barge-in, GPT-4o 0-5 relevance, latency).
   - *unaffected*: CONFIRMED: s1/s3: four dimensions, automatic metrics; authors state metrics are 'intentionally descriptive rather than prescriptive'.
   - anchor `s1 Introduction` @ arXiv:2503.04721v3 (html): "The metrics are intentionally descriptive rather than prescriptive, allowing developers to prioritize behaviors according to specific application requirements."
1. Metrics: Takeover Rate TOR = mean of binary takeover (speech that is neither silence nor a backchannel); Freq = backchannel events/s; JSD between model and human (ICC, ~59 annotators/stimulus, 200 ms windows) backchannel timing; latency in seconds, computed only when TOR=1; interruption GPT-4o judge 0-5. Direction: pause TOR lower-better, smooth-turn TOR and interruption TOR higher-better, latency lower-better. JSD values in Table III span 0.896-0.997, i.e. near the random baseline for every model, so JSD barely discriminates.
   - *narrowed*: Add precise definitions, directions, and that JSD is near-uninformative (0.896-0.997 for all four systems).
   - anchor `s3 definitions (Takeover)` @ arXiv:2503.04721v3 (html): "Conversely, any other non-silent speech that is not a backchannel indicates the model’s attempt to take over."
   - anchor `s3.2.3 Smooth Turn Taking` @ arXiv:2503.04721v3 (html): "The latency is calculated only when TO equals 1."
   - anchor `s3 definitions (Backchannel)` @ arXiv:2503.04721v3 (html): "we classify a speech segment as backchanneling if it meets the following criteria: (1) it has a short duration of less than 1 second; and (2) it contains fewer than two words."
2. Table III (latency seconds): pause TOR synthetic/Candor: dGSLM 0.934/0.935, Moshi 0.985/0.980, Freeze-Omni 0.642/0.481, Gemini Live 0.255/0.310; smooth-turn TOR/latency: dGSLM 0.975/0.352, Moshi 0.941/0.265, Freeze-Omni 0.336/0.953, Gemini 0.655/1.301; interruption TOR/GPT-4o/latency: Moshi 1.000/0.765/0.257, Freeze-Omni 0.867/3.615/1.409, Gemini 0.891/3.376/1.183, dGSLM 0.917/0.201/2.531. Sets: Candor 216 pause + 119 turn-taking, ICC 55 backchannel, synthetic 200 interruption + 137 pause (ChatTTS audio, GPT-4o text).
   - *unaffected*: Numbers verified; note Gemini Live 2.0 is now deprecated and newer API models score far lower pause TOR (repo README).
   - anchor `Table III (Moshi row)` @ arXiv:2503.04721v3 (html): "Moshi | 0.985 | 0.980 | 1.000 | 0.001 | 0.957 | 0.941 | 0.265 | 1.000 | 0.765 | 0.257"
   - anchor `Table III (Gemini Live row)` @ arXiv:2503.04721v3 (html): "Gemini Live | 0.255 | 0.310 | 0.091 | 0.012 | 0.896 | 0.655 | 1.301 | 0.891 | 3.376 | 1.183"
   - anchor `Table III caption` @ arXiv:2503.04721v3 (html): "We evaluate several models across different conversational dimensions, where Latency is presented in seconds."
3. CASCADE SCORABLE: yes in principle. The harness only needs streamed audio in and time-aligned audio out; the paper evaluates Freeze-Omni (a VAD-triggered, 'cascaded' design) and the Gemini Live API next to end-to-end Moshi/dGSLM. A whisper->LLM->qwentts cascade would need a real-time streaming wrapper with VAD/endpointing that writes a time-synchronous output.wav. The cascade will mostly be scored on endpointing quality (a high pause-TOR is the typical failure) rather than on end-to-end modelling.
   - *narrowed*: Successors v1.5/v2/v3 exist (Stage-1 asked about this); data location recorded.
   - anchor `README.md:L18 @ 3e799c4` @ Full-Duplex-Bench@3e799c45a045256f47d5f1c9cda90157e2d2ec9e: "Welcome to **Full-Duplex-Bench**, with v1.0, v1.5, v2.0, and v3.0"
   - anchor `s4 Models Under Evaluation` @ arXiv:2503.04721v3 (html): "Freeze-Omni [10]: A cascaded full-duplex system with a frozen LLM pipeline."
   - anchor `v3/README.md:L124 @ 3e799c4` @ Full-Duplex-Bench@3e799c45a045256f47d5f1c9cda90157e2d2ec9e: "**Option B: Cascaded Agent** (Silero VAD + OpenAI Whisper STT + gpt-4o LLM + OpenAI TTS)"
   - anchor `v3/README.md:L99 @ 3e799c4` @ Full-Duplex-Bench@3e799c45a045256f47d5f1c9cda90157e2d2ec9e: "> **Note:** A [LiveKit Cloud](https://cloud.livekit.io) account (free tier available) is required to run inference."
4. DATA/CODE LOCATION: github.com/DanielLin94144/Full-Duplex-Bench (v1_v1.5/, v2/, v3/); v1 task data (Candor-, ICC- and ChatTTS-derived) is hosted on Google Drive and was not fetched here; the v1 evaluation needs NeMo parakeet-tdt-0.6b-v2 ASR (CPU possible; not tested) and a GPT-4o judge for interruption relevance.
5. SUCCESSORS exist in the same repo (README, commit 3e799c4, 2026-05-20): v1.5 (arXiv 2507.23159, overlap handling: user interruption, listener backchannel, side conversation, ambient speech), v2.0 (arXiv 2510.07838, real-time multi-turn with an automated LLM examiner, Node.js WebRTC orchestrator, Gemini judge), v3.0 (arXiv 2604.04847, tool use under real disfluent speech; ships a cascaded Silero+Whisper+gpt-4o+TTS LiveKit agent but requires a LiveKit Cloud account and OpenAI keys). The repo README now lists newer models (GPT-Realtime 0.010/0.120 pause TOR, Gemini 3.1 Flash Live, PersonaPlex) in the v1 table and marks Gemini Live 2.0 deprecated.

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 Stage-2: all Table III numbers verified; metric definitions and directions written out; cascade scorability settled (yes, black-box; needs streaming wrapper); v1.5/v2/v3 successors identified (titles/dates verified from arXiv abs pages); JSD discrimination weakness noted; v3's cascade agent depends on LiveKit Cloud + OpenAI (not local).

### Verdict

Full-Duplex-Bench v1 is a black-box audio-in/audio-out harness, so it CAN score our whisper+qwentts cascade (it already scores Freeze-Omni and the Gemini API), provided we build a real-time streaming wrapper that emits a time-synchronous output.wav. Use the TOR / smooth-turn-latency / interruption pieces; treat backchannel JSD as noise. v1 is no longer the newest: v1.5 (overlap/side-talk) and v3 (tool use + disfluency + a cascade baseline) are directly relevant to the interlocutor-with-one-tool design and are not yet in the index. Do NOT carry 'Gemini Live ~0.255 pause TOR' as current state of the art; the repo now lists GPT-Realtime 0.010/0.120 and Gemini 3.1 Flash Live 0.007-0.022 / 0.102-0.153.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `handoffs/active/conversation-stack.md`

## intake-2004#record — Qwen3.8-27B on One RTX 3090: 59.8% Faster with MTP, But Not Bit-Exact (Hsiu-Chi Tsai, HackMD)

- **URL**: https://hackmd.io/@thc1006/qwen3-8-27b-speculative-decoding-rtx-3090
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance high; categories: speculative_decoding, local_inference, benchmark_methodology, ssm_hybrid)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-1987#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Own preregistered, archived llama.cpp measurement (master c060ca974, DFlash2 PR #27342 @ d1a522fc): Qwen3.8-27B UD-Q4_K_XL, one stock 420 W RTX 3090, greedy top_k=1, --parallel 1, ctx 8192, q8_0 KV, 25 purposive prompts x 5 passes. MTP n-max 2 = 66.39 vs 41.55 tok/s baseline (+59.8%, nominal 95% CI +57.0..+62.8; author's simulation says real coverage 87.5-92.0%); n-max 2 chosen and scored on the same data.
   - *unaffected*: conditions fully recoverable from post+repo; not comparable to intake-1827#record (patched vLLM, 250 W, W4A16 Marlin, ctx 25k, LABD) or intake-1830#record; do not pool tok/s.
   - anchor `post.md 'The throughput result' table` @ thc1006/qwen3.8-speculative-decoding-rtx3090@705dc2242248fef7f3f297bdb7b6030ec5d52662 (+ post.md sha256 1044009a301278fa...): "| **MTP, `n-max 2`** | 3 | **66.39 tok/s** | **+59.8% [+57.0, +62.8]** | **0.1627** | **2,503** |"
1. Per-class: DFlash2 n-max 7 (verify width 8) is negative on prose -11.1%, chat -4.3%, Chinese -28.7% and positive on code +91.8%, reasoning +65.4%; the shallower DFlash2 n-max 4 (width 5) is positive on all five classes and beats MTP n-max 2 on code (+116.8 vs +92.0) and reasoning (+89.4 vs +73.6) but loses on prose/chat/zh. Depth, not family, drives the sign. No paired MTP-vs-DFlash2 winner is declared; class effects are 5 prompts each.
   - *narrowed*: the DFlash2 losses are specific to n-max 7 / width 8; Stage-1 phrasing 'MTP outperforms DFlash2' mixes widths. Consistent in direction with intake-1830#record's author caveat, still not a replication.
   - anchor `post.md 'The average hides a workload sign change' table` @ thc1006/qwen3.8-speculative-decoding-rtx3090@705dc2242248fef7f3f297bdb7b6030ec5d52662 (+ post.md sha256 1044009a301278fa...): "| **DFlash2, `n-max 7`** | +91.8% | +65.4% | **−11.1%** | **−4.3%** | **−28.7%** |"
2. Speculative output is not byte-identical to the same tree's non-speculative greedy output: at a 1600-token cap 92-100% of the 125 prompt-passes per arm diverge (first-differing character of stored text vs baseline), deterministically across passes and across same-build reruns (150/150). Fork positions group by verification width ({3,4} vs {5,6,8}) across MTP and DFlash2 alike, a warp-count intervention changed kernel speed but no output byte, and BF16 weights on a 9B sibling still diverge on 36/75 - so the cause is verify-batch numerics of unestablished mechanism, not DFlash2 and not quantization alone. Answer quality was not measured.
   - *narrowed*: 'cause not established' is too weak: drafter-independence and width partition are measured; 'quantized target' framing too narrow since BF16 (9B) also diverges; metric definition now precise. Repo itself says it corroborates llama.cpp #25618 rather than discovering anything.
   - anchor `docs/GREEDY_DIVERGENCE.md 'The partition is by verification width, not by drafter'` @ thc1006/qwen3.8-speculative-decoding-rtx3090@705dc2242248fef7f3f297bdb7b6030ec5d52662 (+ post.md sha256 1044009a301278fa...): "The grouping crosses drafters**: width 5 and width 8 are DFlash2 while width 6 is the built-in MTP head, and all three agree with each other on every prompt."
3. An external 0.8B drafter on dense Qwen3.8 lost 29.8% (-33.1..-26.4); the earlier Qwen3.6-35B-A3B result (every llama.cpp configuration lost) is the author's own prior study, not re-read.
   - *unaffected*: confirmed from post text.
   - anchor `post.md 'Why did the answer flip'` @ thc1006/qwen3.8-speculative-decoding-rtx3090@705dc2242248fef7f3f297bdb7b6030ec5d52662 (+ post.md sha256 1044009a301278fa...): "It still lost: **−29.8% [−33.1%, −26.4%]**."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 Stage-2: read raw post.md (26.6 KB) and cloned repo @705dc22 (MIT). Precisely defined the divergence metric and its arms; found the repo attributes it to verify-batch width (drafter-independent, warp-count refuted as cause, BF16 9B still diverges 36/75), narrowing Stage-1 'cause not established'; found DFlash2 n-max 4 is positive on every class and above MTP n2 on code/reasoning, narrowing the per-workload claim to the n-max 7 point.

### Verdict

Take away: (1) an independent-hardware, preregistered llama.cpp measurement that speculative verify batches are not byte-identical to serial greedy on a quantized 27B, drafter-independent and grouped by verify width - this matches what OUR DF2-6/DF2-8 already found on MI210 (not a DFlash2 defect) and adds a width-partition test we have not run; (2) DFlash2 per-class sign depends on n-max (n7 loses prose/chat/zh on 3090 Q4_K_XL ctx 8k; n4 does not). Do NOT carry: the +59.8% or any tok/s onto MI210/Q8_0/ctx 196k (different kernels: our Q8_0 verify takes the MMQ route), do NOT cite it as a replication of intake-1827#record/1830, do NOT say divergence is quantization-caused or DFlash2-caused, do NOT say anything about answer quality (unmeasured). Author is a single individual; the repo is unusually transparent (append-only corrections) but unreviewed.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `handoffs/active/dflash2-block-drafter-experimental-build.md`
- updated `handoffs/active/qwen38-27b-replace-qwen36.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): [unverified] Cite as intake_entry in handoffs/active/dflash2-block-drafter-experimental-build.md as corroboration of workload-dependence only; do not pool its tok/s with HyperQwen figures.
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): [unverified] Add divergence-from-serial-greedy rate as a recorded metric in the spec-decode verification (llama.cpp issue #25618 referenced by the post).

## intake-2005#record — LLM4LLM: Bridging Kernel Benchmarks and Real Deployment via Closed-Loop Agentic Optimization

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21836
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: autonomous_research, hardware_optimization, benchmark_methodology)
- **Disposition**: monitor
- **Expanded from**: intake-1998#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Isolated kernel benchmarks can misrank candidates against in-model behaviour; the paper supports this with three qualitative cases (cold-vs-warm cache reversal on a conv layer, a latent OOB read that only faults inside the full-model allocator, and a prefill-specialized attention kernel inapplicable to decode) but reports no aggregate gap rate.
   - *narrowed*: Gap is demonstrated by anecdotal cases, not measured as a population statistic; the size is "a substantial speedup" turning into an end-to-end slowdown for one conv layer.
   - anchor `§2.1` @ arXiv:2608.21836v1 (html, sha256 66a29cc3...6e09); code hzeng2000/LLM4LLM@4e51e82cc3a242f3f76f29645fcfb5d66335c518 (MIT): "The isolated benchmark reports a substantial speedup, while the integrated model becomes slower end to end."
1. LLM4LLM = single-script, single-GPU closed loop: profile the target inference script, score modules by phase-weighted time share, build separate prefill/decode tasks with a dispatch template, search in episodes with restart + distilled experience, and accept a patch only after in-model correctness and end-to-end latency improve by at least delta. Measures batch-1 script latency vs eager PyTorch, not a serving stack (no continuous batching, TP/PP or multi-tenant; stated as limits).
   - *unaffected*: Verified incl. phase-weighted hotspot score and delta threshold; add: single-script batch-1 latency vs eager, not a serving stack.
   - anchor `§3.3 Eq.3 context` @ arXiv:2608.21836v1 (html, sha256 66a29cc3...6e09); code hzeng2000/LLM4LLM@4e51e82cc3a242f3f76f29645fcfb5d66335c518 (MIT): "Each candidate is inserted into the target model instance and evaluated for in-context correctness and latency."
2. Results (NVIDIA only, A100-SXM4-80GB and H100-80GB): geometric mean end-to-end speedup over EAGER PyTorch across ten workloads is 3.91x (A100) and 6.98x (H100) (recomputed from Table 1: 3.908, 6.981), dominated by Mamba (up to 32.8x) and RecurrentGemma; transformer rows are 1.24x-3.78x. Anti-reward-hacking for KernelBench is delegated to the benchmark harness; no AMD or CPU target.
   - *unaffected*: Confirmed by arXiv abs Comments field.
   - anchor `§4.1 setup` @ arXiv:2608.21836v1 (html, sha256 66a29cc3...6e09); code hzeng2000/LLM4LLM@4e51e82cc3a242f3f76f29645fcfb5d66335c518 (MIT): "LLM4LLM starts optimization from the eager PyTorch execution path."
   - anchor `§4.4 exploit handling` @ arXiv:2608.21836v1 (html, sha256 66a29cc3...6e09); code hzeng2000/LLM4LLM@4e51e82cc3a242f3f76f29645fcfb5d66335c518 (MIT): "LLM4LLM delegates exploit detection in isolated KernelBench runs to the benchmark harness"
3. Restart ablation (KernelBench L2, 100 tasks, single run per cell): full vs w/o restart geomean vs eager GPT-5.4 2.005 vs 1.961 (10 trials) / 2.153 vs 1.973 (15), Claude Sonnet 4.6 2.447 vs 2.267 / 2.546 vs 2.417, GLM-5 2.539 vs 2.179 / 2.745 vs 2.294; the headline 2.745x is GLM-5 at 15 trials, A100. Restart here keeps distilled constraints (opposite of intake-1998#record which keeps nothing but the best candidate). Accepted to EMNLP 2026 main (abs comments).
   - anchor `§4.5 Ablation` @ arXiv:2608.21836v1 (html, sha256 66a29cc3...6e09); code hzeng2000/LLM4LLM@4e51e82cc3a242f3f76f29645fcfb5d66335c518 (MIT): "Comparing “w/o restart” with full LLM4LLM across all three models shows that restart contributes beyond a larger optimization budget, supporting the episodic design in Section 3.2."
   - anchor `§6 Conclusion limits` @ arXiv:2608.21836v1 (html, sha256 66a29cc3...6e09); code hzeng2000/LLM4LLM@4e51e82cc3a242f3f76f29645fcfb5d66335c518 (MIT): "LLM4LLM currently targets single-GPU inference workloads and assumes access to a representative inference script."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 Stage-2: full HTML read + repo cloned (MIT, @4e51e82). Confirmed 3.91x/6.98x by recomputing the geomeans from Table 1 (3.908/6.981) but the baseline is eager PyTorch and the ten workloads include Mamba rows up to 32.8x. The bench-vs-deployment gap is qualitative (three cases), not a measured rate. Reward-hack handling is delegated to the KernelBench harness. NVIDIA-only; single-script batch-1 latency, no serving stack. Restart ablation numbers extracted: restart gains +2% to +20% in GeoMean vs eager, largest for GLM-5. Credibility 4->3, relevance high->medium.

### Verdict

Take away: the qualitative taxonomy (cold-vs-warm cache reversal, latent OOB that only faults in the real allocator, prefill-only kernels not covering decode) and the rule "accept only on in-model correctness + end-to-end latency" are consistent with AK design; the restart-with-distilled-notes ablation is the one transferable measurement. Do NOT carry: 3.91x/6.98x as evidence about llama.cpp/HIP/CPU (baseline is naive eager PyTorch, Mamba rows inflate the geomean, NVIDIA only); any claim that the gap magnitude is quantified; reward-hacking robustness (delegated to KernelBench harness, nothing of its own beyond tolerance + safety checks).
